Hi, Doctor. Thank you so much for fitting me in, I really appreciate it. I hope I'm not taking up too much of your time. 

Um, well, I guess I'm here because I've been having this silly little issue with my conversations lately. It's probably nothing major, honestly, just stress or something, but... well, I work as a jeweler, and recently, if a customer asks me a very simple question—like, um, when a ring repair will be ready to pick up—I try to answer them, but my words just sort of... go on a little detour. 

I'll start explaining about the repair invoices, and then somehow that turns into talking about garden soil, and then suddenly I'm bringing up my mortgage papers... and I can just see the look on their face, like I've completely lost them. It's terribly embarrassing. I try so hard to be professional and give them a good experience, you know? 

Does... does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining it right for you.

Oh, well... I mean, it's really not a huge deal, I promise. I'm still working and keeping busy! But, um... I suppose I have had to step back just a tiny bit from my custom pieces lately. Just temporarily, of course!

Oh, um... well, no, not really. I mean, nothing major, certainly nothing crazy! I know how that sounds, and I promise I'm perfectly grounded in reality. 

It's just... well, sometimes when the shop is very quiet—you know how old buildings settle and make noises?—sometimes I'll hear what sounds like a woman sort of muttering my name. Just from the polishing room in the back. And occasionally, I'll catch a whiff of burning metal, even when I know for a fact all the torches are completely off. 

It's probably just my imagination playing tricks on me, or the ventilation acting up. I always go back there to check, just to be safe, because it feels so real in the moment. But I know it's just my mind being a little overtired. That's completely normal when you work long hours, right? I'm sure lots of people experience little things like that when they're stressed. I hope you don't think I'm losing my touch or anything.

Oh, um... well, it definitely sounds like it's coming from outside of me. Like I was saying, it really sounds like it's coming from right over in the polishing room. That's... that's why I always get up to go check. Because it feels so incredibly real in the moment, like there's actually someone standing in the back, or like a real torch was left burning. 

If I thought it was just inside my own head, I probably wouldn't bother getting up from my workbench to look, right? But I promise, once I check and see it's empty, I know it's just the acoustics of the building playing tricks on me. I hope that makes sense? I really don't want you to think I'm losing my mind over a drafty old shop.

Oh, um... well, mostly, yes. I try very hard to be clear. But... well, actually, that's sort of why I thought I should come in. It's embarrassing to admit, but sometimes I do notice people looking a bit lost when I'm talking. 

Like, if a customer asks a simple question—just something like when their ring repair will be ready—I'll start to answer, and then somehow I'm talking about the invoices... and then maybe how the garden soil needs turning, and then suddenly I'm mentioning the mortgage papers. In my head, it feels like all those things are connected in the moment, but when I see their expressions, I realize I must sound a bit scattered. I always try to pull it back and apologize, of course. I just want to be helpful to them. It's just stress making my thoughts jump around a little bit, right? I really hope I'm not making a terrible impression on people.

I hope I'm explaining it right for you.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble with your words going off on a detour like that all the time, or does it just happen sometimes?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's not all the time, I don't think. I mean, I really try to keep things brief and professional. But yes, like I was saying, it's just so strange when it happens. A customer will just ask a quick question about when their ring repair will be ready, and I really mean to just give them a time, but... my answer just starts going from the invoices, to the garden soil, to the mortgage papers. \n\nAnd I can just see them losing me, you know? They get this polite but confused look. I feel terrible about it, I really don't want to make them uncomfortable or seem like I don't know what I'm doing. I'm usually very good at my job, I promise. Do you think... is that something you see a lot? I hope it's not too unusual.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are connected when you're talking, even if your words seem to go off on a detour?\n\nPatient: Um, well... to me, in my head, it does feel connected at the time. I'm really not trying to be confusing, I promise. It's just... I'll start thinking about the invoices for the repair, and then somehow that reminds me of the yard work I need to pay for, which brings up the soil, and then the mortgage... and before I know it, that's what I'm saying out loud to the poor customer who just wanted to know about their ring. \n\nIt makes sense to me while it's happening, but then I see their faces and I realize I've completely derailed. It's just a silly habit I've developed, I suppose. I'm trying very hard to catch myself. Does that... does that answer your question, Doctor? I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like this issue with your conversations is affecting other areas of your life or relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I mean, it's really not a huge deal, I promise. I'm still working and keeping busy! But, um... I suppose I have had to step back just a tiny bit from my custom pieces lately. Just temporarily, of course! \n\nIt's just that I was, well, missing a couple of repair deadlines here and there, and I'd sort of lose track of what we were talking about during client consultations... which is just terribly unprofessional of me, I know. I really don't want to give anyone a bad experience. And my husband—he's so sweet—he's actually been double-checking my invoices for me recently. I always used to handle all the paperwork myself without any issues, but it's just... it's been nice to have a second pair of eyes while I'm a little scattered. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound completely incapable. I'm really trying my best to stay on top of everything, Doctor. Is it okay that I'm leaning on him a little bit right now?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear or see things that other people do not?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, no, not really. I mean, nothing major, certainly nothing crazy! I know how that sounds, and I promise I'm perfectly grounded in reality. \n\nIt's just... well, sometimes when the shop is very quiet—you know how old buildings settle and make noises?—sometimes I'll hear what sounds like a woman sort of muttering my name. Just from the polishing room in the back. And occasionally, I'll catch a whiff of burning metal, even when I know for a fact all the torches are completely off. \n\nIt's probably just my imagination playing tricks on me, or the ventilation acting up. I always go back there to check, just to be safe, because it feels so real in the moment. But I know it's just my mind being a little overtired. That's completely normal when you work long hours, right? I'm sure lots of people experience little things like that when they're stressed. I hope you don't think I'm losing my touch or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the voices you hear, like the woman muttering your name, are coming from inside your head or from outside?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, it definitely sounds like it's coming from outside of me. Like I was saying, it really sounds like it's coming from right over in the polishing room. That's... that's why I always get up to go check. Because it feels so incredibly real in the moment, like there's actually someone standing in the back, or like a real torch was left burning. \n\nIf I thought it was just inside my own head, I probably wouldn't bother getting up from my workbench to look, right? But I promise, once I check and see it's empty, I know it's just the acoustics of the building playing tricks on me. I hope that makes sense? I really don't want you to think I'm losing my mind over a drafty old shop.\n\nDoctor: How often do you hear the woman muttering your name or smell burning metal when you're alone in the shop?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... it's really not very often at all, I promise! Just... just every once in a while. Maybe a couple of times a week? And only when it's very late and I've been staring at the same setting for hours. \n\nIt's not like it's a constant distraction or anything that's keeping me from my work. It's just a fleeting little thing, and then I check, and it's fine, and I go right back to what I was doing. I really try not to let it bother me. That doesn't sound too concerning, does it? I mean, a few times a week when you're overtired is probably pretty standard for most people, wouldn't you say?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to understand and follow your own train of thought when you're speaking, even if others seem confused?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, mostly, yes. I try very hard to be clear. But... well, actually, that's sort of why I thought I should come in. It's embarrassing to admit, but sometimes I do notice people looking a bit lost when I'm talking. \n\nLike, if a customer asks a simple question—just something like when their ring repair will be ready—I'll start to answer, and then somehow I'm talking about the invoices... and then maybe how the garden soil needs turning, and then suddenly I'm mentioning the mortgage papers. And I can just see it in their faces, you know? They're completely losing me. \n\nIn my head, it feels like all those things are connected in the moment, but when I see their expressions, I realize I must sound a bit scattered. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, goodness, I know I just gave you that exact example about the ring repairs and the garden soil... I'm so sorry, I must sound like a broken record! I hope I'm not frustrating you by repeating myself. 

To answer your question, um... I'd like to think it's still just occasional. I really do try my hardest to keep my conversations on track, I promise. It's definitely not every single time I speak to someone. 

But... well, it happens enough that it's really the whole reason I came in to see you today. Just those moments where a customer asks a simple question, and my answer just spirals from the shop invoices all the way to the mortgage papers, and I can physically see them losing me. I hate making people feel confused when I'm just trying to be helpful. I really do try my best to be clear... it's just a little occasional, harmless rambling, right? I'm sure plenty of people do that.

I just see their faces kind of go blank, and I realize I've completely lost them. It's so embarrassing. I always apologize and try to get back on track. I really hope it doesn't make me sound foolish.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're having trouble sticking to the point in your conversations most of the time, or is it still just occasional?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, I know I just gave you that exact example about the ring repairs and the garden soil... I really do try my hardest to keep my conversations on track, I promise. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... racing? I wouldn't say racing. That sounds a bit... well, a bit extreme, doesn't it? I really don't want you to think I'm completely out of control. 

It's just... oh dear, I know I'm bringing up the exact same example again. I am so sorry, you must be getting so tired of hearing about the shop! But it really is the whole reason I came in to see you. When they ask that simple question about the ring repair, the thoughts about the invoices and the garden soil and the mortgage... they don't feel fast or scary in my head. They just sort of tumble out because they all feel perfectly connected to me in that second. 

It's only when I see them getting lost that I realize I didn't quite control how it came out, and it got all jumbled. I really am trying my best to keep things simple for them, I promise. Does that make sense? I hope I'm answering your question the right way...

Oh, um... my mood? I... well, I suppose everyone gets a little down sometimes, right? I really hope you don't think I'm just sitting around feeling sorry for myself. I try so hard to keep a positive attitude, especially for the customers.

I really am trying my best to keep things simple for them, I promise. Does that make sense? I hope I'm answering your question the right way...\n\nDoctor: Do you think the way your thoughts seem to jump from one topic to another is related to any changes in your mood or emotional state?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... my mood? I... well, I suppose everyone gets a little down sometimes, right? I really hope you don't think I'm just sitting around feeling sorry for myself. I try so hard to keep a positive attitude, especially for the customers. \n\nBut... well, if I'm being completely honest—and I really want to give you the right answers—I have been feeling a bit... low. It's not a big deal, really, I'm sure it's just a phase. But most mornings lately, I wake up with this... this flat, heavy sadness. It's almost like all the color has just drained out of the house before I even leave for the shop. \n\nI try to shake it off, I promise I do. I don't want to be a burden or bring anyone else down. Do you think... do you think feeling a little blue like that could be making me jumble my words up? I just want to make sure I'm giving you what you need to figure this out. I'm doing okay, though, really.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow long have you been waking up with that flat, heavy sadness in the mornings?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 53 / jeweler_or_precious_stone_or_metal_worker / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Depressive Episode Duration\nmanifestation: \"The depression has been with me for about a year and four months, since that long stretch when the mortgage pressure started feeling impossible.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... well, when you ask how long... I really don't want it to sound like I've just been wallowing, you know? I do try my absolute hardest to push through it every single day, I promise. 

But, um... if I'm being completely honest with you... I suppose it's been with me for about a year and four months now? Give or take a little. It started back during this really long stretch when the pressure from the shop's mortgage just started feeling... well, a bit impossible to keep up with. 

I feel terrible admitting it's been going on for that long without me just fixing it myself. I really should have snapped out of it by now, shouldn't I? I hope you don't think I'm just weak or not trying hard enough to be happy. I really am trying my best to handle it all... is it okay that it's been that long? I hope I'm not a hopeless case or anything.

I really don't want to give anyone a bad experience. And my husband—he's so sweet—he's actually been double-checking my invoices for me recently. But I promise, once I check and see it's empty, I know it's just the acoustics of the building playing tricks on me. I hope that makes sense? I really don't want you to think I'm losing my mind over a drafty old shop.\n\nDoctor: How often do you hear the woman muttering your name or smell burning metal when you're alone in the shop?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... it's really not very often at all, I promise! Just... just every once in a while. Maybe a couple of times a week? And only when it's very late and I've been staring at the same setting for hours. \n\nIt's not like it's a constant distraction or anything that's keeping me from my work. It's just a fleeting little thing, and then I check, and it's fine, and I go right back to what I was doing. I really try not to let it bother me. That doesn't sound too concerning, does it? I mean, a few times a week when you're overtired is probably pretty standard for most people, wouldn't you say?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to understand and follow your own train of thought when you're speaking, even if others seem confused?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, mostly, yes. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... well, like I was saying just a moment ago... I really do try to put on a brave face, you know? I don't want to be a bother to anyone, especially the customers at the jewelry shop. 

But... no, it doesn't really improve. I wake up most mornings with that flat, heavy sadness... the one where it feels like all the color has just drained right out of the house before I even leave for work. And it just sort of... stays. I hope you don't think I'm just repeating myself to be difficult, I just... I want to make sure I'm answering your question the right way. I really do try my best to be cheerful, though. Is it okay that I feel that way?

But I promise, once I check and see it's empty, I know it's just the acoustics of the building playing tricks on me. I hope that makes sense? I really don't want you to think I'm losing my mind over a drafty old shop.\n\nDoctor: How often do you hear the woman muttering your name or smell burning metal when you're alone in the shop?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... it's really not very often at all, I promise! Just... just every once in a while. Maybe a couple of times a week? And only when it's very late and I've been staring at the same setting for hours. \n\nIt's not like it's a constant distraction or anything that's keeping me from my work. It's just a fleeting little thing, and then I check, and it's fine, and I go right back to what I was doing. I really try not to let it bother me. That doesn't sound too concerning, does it? I mean, a few times a week when you're overtired is probably pretty standard for most people, wouldn't you say?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to understand and follow your own train of thought when you're speaking, even if others seem confused?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, mostly, yes. I try very hard to be clear. But... well, actually, that's sort of why I thought I should come in. It's embarrassing to admit, but sometimes I do notice people looking a bit lost when I'm talking. \n\nLike, if a customer asks a simple question—just something like when their ring repair will be ready—I'll start to answer, and then somehow I'm talking about the invoices... and then maybe how the garden soil needs turning, and then suddenly I'm mentioning the mortgage papers. And I can just see it in their faces, you know? They're completely losing me. \n\nIn my head, it feels like all those things are connected in the moment, but when I see their expressions, I realize I must sound a bit scattered. I always try to pull it back and apologize, of course. I just want to be helpful to them. It's just stress making my thoughts jump around a little bit, right? I really hope I'm not making a terrible impression on people.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble expressing your thoughts in a coherent and logical manner, even when you're not feeling stressed or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, it's mostly just when things are busy, I think. I mean, I really try to be organized. But I guess... well, like I was saying before, that's kind of the main reason I decided to make an appointment. \n\nEven when I'm just sitting at the counter and it's quiet, a customer might ask something so simple, like... like when their ring repair will be ready. And I start to tell them Tuesday, but then I'm suddenly talking about the invoices on my desk, and then somehow I'm mentioning that the garden soil needs turning before it freezes, and... and then I'm talking about the mortgage papers. \n\nI don't mean to do it, I promise. Oh, um... well, I really try to keep busy, I promise I do. I don't want to sound like I'm just sitting around the shop doing nothing all day. I'm usually very dedicated to my work. 

But... well, since you're asking about things I used to enjoy... I feel terrible admitting this, but... I used to just love hunting for old, vintage clasps, you know? I'd love to tell you that it lifts after a nice cup of tea or when I start organizing the displays. But... well, it's just like I was trying to say before. Most mornings I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness... it's like the color has just drained out of the house before I even get to the shop. And I'm afraid it just stays that heavy, even though I try my absolute hardest to look on the bright side for the rest of the afternoon. \n\nI'm so sorry to be such a broken record about it. Am I making sense now? People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, dear, I'm so sorry. I must have mumbled or not been clear enough just a moment ago. I really don't mean to make you ask the same thing twice, Doctor. 

But, um, yes, like I was trying to say... I used to get so much joy out of hunting for old clasps and planning those little jewelry classes. I really prided myself on it. But lately... well, I mostly just find myself staring at the trays of beads. It just feels like none of it is really worth starting right now. 

I feel so guilty even saying it out loud. I know I should be more grateful for my work. Am I giving you the right kind of information? I really just want to make sure I'm helping you figure this all out.

They're completely losing me. \n\nIn my head, it feels like all those things are connected in the moment, but when I see their expressions, I realize I must sound a bit scattered. I really hope I'm not making a terrible impression on people.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble expressing your thoughts in a coherent and logical manner, even when you're not feeling stressed or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, it's mostly just when things are busy, I think. Oh, um... weight? Well, I don't want you to think I'm not taking care of myself. I really do try to keep up with things, I promise. I know how important it is to eat right.

They get this polite but confused look. I feel terrible about it, I really don't want to make them uncomfortable or seem like I don't know what I'm doing. I'm usually very good at my job, I promise. Do you think... is that something you see a lot? I hope it's not too unusual.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are connected when you're talking, even if your words seem to go off on a detour?\n\nPatient: Um, well... to me, in my head, it does feel connected at the time. I'm really not trying to be confusing, I promise. It's just... I'll start thinking about the invoices for the repair, and then somehow that reminds me of the yard work I need to pay for, which brings up the soil, and then the mortgage... and before I know it, that's what I'm saying out loud to the poor customer who just wanted to know about their ring. \n\nIt makes sense to me while it's happening, but then I see their faces and I realize I've completely derailed. It's just a silly habit I've developed, I suppose. I'm trying very hard to catch myself. Does that... does that answer your question, Doctor? I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like this issue with your conversations is affecting other areas of your life or relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I mean, it's really not a huge deal, I promise. I'm still working and keeping busy! But, um... I suppose I have had to step back just a tiny bit from my custom pieces lately. Just temporarily, of course! \n\nIt's just that I was, well, missing a couple of repair deadlines here and there, and I'd sort of lose track of what we were talking about during client consultations... which is just terribly unprofessional of me, I know. I really don't want to give anyone a bad experience. And my husband—he's so sweet—he's actually been double-checking my invoices for me recently. I always used to handle all the paperwork myself without any issues, but it's just... it's been nice to have a second pair of eyes while I'm a little scattered. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound completely incapable. I'm really trying my best to stay on top of everything, Doctor. Is it okay that I'm leaning on him a little bit right now?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear or see things that other people do not?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, no, not really. I mean, nothing major, certainly nothing crazy! I know how that sounds, and I promise I'm perfectly grounded in reality. \n\nIt's just... well, sometimes when the shop is very quiet—you know how old buildings settle and make noises?—sometimes I'll hear what sounds like a woman sort of muttering my name. Just from the polishing room in the back. And occasionally, I'll catch a whiff of burning metal, even when I know for a fact all the torches are completely off. \n\nIt's probably just my imagination playing tricks on me, or the ventilation acting up. I always go back there to check, just to be safe, because it feels so real in the moment. But I know it's just my mind being a little overtired. That's completely normal when you work long hours, right? I'm sure lots of people experience little things like that when they're stressed. I hope you don't think I'm losing my touch or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the voices you hear, like the woman muttering your name, are coming from inside your head or from outside?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, it definitely sounds like it's coming from outside of me. Like I was saying, it really sounds like it's coming from right over in the polishing room. That's... that's why I always get up to go check. Because it feels so incredibly real in the moment, like there's actually someone standing in the back, or like a real torch was left burning. \n\nIf I thought it was just inside my own head, I probably wouldn't bother getting up from my workbench to look, right? But I promise, once I check and see it's empty, I know it's just the acoustics of the building playing tricks on me. I hope that makes sense? I really don't want you to think I'm losing my mind over a drafty old shop.\n\nDoctor: How often do you hear the woman muttering your name or smell burning metal when you're alone in the shop?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... it's really not very often at all, I promise! Just... just every once in a while. Maybe a couple of times a week? And only when it's very late and I've been staring at the same setting for hours. \n\nIt's not like it's a constant distraction or anything that's keeping me from my work. It's just a fleeting little thing, and then I check, and it's fine, and I go right back to what I was doing. I really try not to let it bother me. That doesn't sound too concerning, does it? I mean, a few times a week when you're overtired is probably pretty standard for most people, wouldn't you say?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to understand and follow your own train of thought when you're speaking, even if others seem confused?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, mostly, yes. I try very hard to be clear. But... well, actually, that's sort of why I thought I should come in. It's embarrassing to admit, but sometimes I do notice people looking a bit lost when I'm talking. \n\nLike, if a customer asks a simple question—just something like when their ring repair will be ready—I'll start to answer, and then somehow I'm talking about the invoices... and then maybe how the garden soil needs turning, and then suddenly I'm mentioning the mortgage papers. And I can just see it in their faces, you know? They're completely losing me. \n\nIn my head, it feels like all those things are connected in the moment, but when I see their expressions, I realize I must sound a bit scattered. I always try to pull it back and apologize, of course. I just want to be helpful to them. It's just stress making my thoughts jump around a little bit, right? I really hope I'm not making a terrible impression on people.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble expressing your thoughts in a coherent and logical manner, even when you're not feeling stressed or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, it's mostly just when things are busy, I think. I mean, I really try to be organized. But I guess... well, like I was saying before, that's kind of the main reason I decided to make an appointment. \n\nEven when I'm just sitting at the counter and it's quiet, a customer might ask something so simple, like... like when their ring repair will be ready. And I start to tell them Tuesday, but then I'm suddenly talking about the invoices on my desk, and then somehow I'm mentioning that the garden soil needs turning before it freezes, and... and then I'm talking about the mortgage papers. \n\nI don't mean to do it, I promise. I just see their faces kind of go blank, and I realize I've completely lost them. It's so embarrassing. I always apologize and try to get back on track. I just want to give them good customer service, you know? It's probably just normal aging, right? Or just having a lot on my plate? I really hope it doesn't make me sound foolish.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're having trouble sticking to the point in your conversations most of the time, or is it still just occasional?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, I know I just gave you that exact example about the ring repairs and the garden soil... I'm so sorry, I must sound like a broken record! I hope I'm not frustrating you by repeating myself. \n\nTo answer your question, um... I'd like to think it's still just occasional. I really do try my hardest to keep my conversations on track, I promise. It's definitely not every single time I speak to someone. \n\nBut... well, it happens enough that it's really the whole reason I came in to see you today. Just those moments where a customer asks a simple question, and my answer just spirals from the shop invoices all the way to the mortgage papers, and I can physically see them losing me. I hate making people feel confused when I'm just trying to be helpful. I really do try my best to be clear... it's just a little occasional, harmless rambling, right? I'm sure plenty of people do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are racing or difficult to control when you're having these conversations that go off on a detour?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... racing? I wouldn't say racing. That sounds a bit... well, a bit extreme, doesn't it? I really don't want you to think I'm completely out of control. \n\nIt's just... oh dear, I know I'm bringing up the exact same example again. I am so sorry, you must be getting so tired of hearing about the shop! But it really is the whole reason I came in to see you. When they ask that simple question about the ring repair, the thoughts about the invoices and the garden soil and the mortgage... they don't feel fast or scary in my head. They just sort of tumble out because they all feel perfectly connected to me in that second. \n\nIt's only when I see them getting lost that I realize I didn't quite control how it came out, and it got all jumbled. I really am trying my best to keep things simple for them, I promise. Does that make sense? I hope I'm answering your question the right way...\n\nDoctor: Do you think the way your thoughts seem to jump from one topic to another is related to any changes in your mood or emotional state?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... my mood? I... well, I suppose everyone gets a little down sometimes, right? I really hope you don't think I'm just sitting around feeling sorry for myself. I try so hard to keep a positive attitude, especially for the customers. \n\nBut... well, if I'm being completely honest—and I really want to give you the right answers—I have been feeling a bit... low. It's not a big deal, really, I'm sure it's just a phase. But most mornings lately, I wake up with this... this flat, heavy sadness. It's almost like all the color has just drained out of the house before I even leave for the shop. \n\nI try to shake it off, I promise I do. I don't want to be a burden or bring anyone else down. Do you think... do you think feeling a little blue like that could be making me jumble my words up? I just want to make sure I'm giving you what you need to figure this out. I'm doing okay, though, really.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been waking up with that flat, heavy sadness in the mornings?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, when you ask how long... I really don't want it to sound like I've just been wallowing, you know? I do try my absolute hardest to push through it every single day, I promise. \n\nBut, um... if I'm being completely honest with you... I suppose it's been with me for about a year and four months now? Give or take a little. It started back during this really long stretch when the pressure from the shop's mortgage just started feeling... well, a bit impossible to keep up with. \n\nI feel terrible admitting it's been going on for that long without me just fixing it myself. I really should have snapped out of it by now, shouldn't I? I hope you don't think I'm just weak or not trying hard enough to be happy. I really am trying my best to handle it all... is it okay that it's been that long? I hope I'm not a hopeless case or anything.\n\nDoctor: Does the flat, heavy sadness you wake up with in the mornings tend to stay with you throughout the day, or does it lift at some point?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I really try not to let it ruin the whole day. I wouldn't want you to think I just drag my feet around the shop from morning till night, feeling miserable. I put on a smile for the customers, I really do. \n\nBut... well, since you're asking, and I want to be a good patient and give you the right answer... that flat, heavy sadness I mentioned? The one where it feels like the color is just drained out of everything? It... it doesn't really go away. I mean, I push through it! I keep working and I do my job. But underneath, it just sort of sits there with me all day long. \n\nI'm so sorry, I know that sounds awful, and I really should be able to cheer myself up by lunchtime, right? I hope it doesn't make me sound terribly gloomy or ungrateful for the things I do have. I just... I can't seem to shake it off, no matter how hard I try. Does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining it alright for you.\n\nDoctor: Does your sadness ever improve at any point during the day?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, like I was saying just a moment ago... I really do try to put on a brave face, you know? I don't want to be a bother to anyone, especially the customers at the jewelry shop. \n\nBut... no, it doesn't really improve. I wake up most mornings with that flat, heavy sadness... the one where it feels like all the color has just drained right out of the house before I even leave for work. And it just sort of... stays. I hope you don't think I'm just repeating myself to be difficult, I just... I want to make sure I'm answering your question the right way. I really do try my best to be cheerful, though. Is it okay that I feel that way?\n\nDoctor: Does there ever come a point in the day where you feel like the sadness lifts or becomes less heavy, even if it's just for a little while?\n\nPatient: Oh, I'm so sorry, Doctor. I must be doing a terrible job of explaining things if you're having to ask me again. I feel awful making you repeat yourself. \n\nI really wish I could give you a happier answer, I truly do. I'd love to tell you that it lifts after a nice cup of tea or when I start organizing the displays. But... well, it's just like I was trying to say before. Most mornings I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness... it's like the color has just drained out of the house before I even get to the shop. And I'm afraid it just stays that heavy, even though I try my absolute hardest to look on the bright side for the rest of the afternoon. \n\nI'm so sorry to be such a broken record about it. Am I making sense now? I really just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to enjoy activities you used to like despite feeling sad?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I really try to keep busy, I promise I do. I don't want to sound like I'm just sitting around the shop doing nothing all day. I'm usually very dedicated to my work. \n\nBut... well, since you're asking about things I used to enjoy... I feel terrible admitting this, but... I used to just love hunting for old, vintage clasps, you know? And I used to spend hours planning these little jewelry-making classes for the community. It was always so much fun. But lately... oh, I hope this doesn't sound too lazy of me... lately, I just find myself mostly staring at the trays of beads and stones. It just feels like none of it is really worth starting right now. \n\nI'm so sorry, I know I should be more enthusiastic about my trade. I really hope you don't think poorly of me for letting things slide a bit. Does that answer your question okay? I'm really trying to give you exactly what you need to know.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear, I'm so sorry. I must have mumbled or not been clear enough just a moment ago. I really don't mean to make you ask the same thing twice, Doctor. \n\nBut, um, yes, like I was trying to say... I used to get so much joy out of hunting for old clasps and planning those little jewelry classes. I really prided myself on it. But lately... well, I mostly just find myself staring at the trays of beads. It just feels like none of it is really worth starting right now. \n\nI feel so guilty even saying it out loud. I know I should be more grateful for my work. Am I giving you the right kind of information? I really just want to make sure I'm helping you figure this all out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... weight? Well, I don't want you to think I'm not taking care of myself. I really do try to keep up with things, I promise. I know how important it is to eat right. \n\nBut... I suppose, if I'm being completely honest... my appetite has been a little bit off. For a few months now, really. I feel awful admitting it, because I know I should be cooking proper meals, but... some days I'll get to the evening and realize I've only had a cup of coffee and maybe a few crackers. It's not that I'm trying to lose weight or anything silly like that! It's just... lately, the thought of preparing food and eating it... it just seems like another huge task that I don't have the energy for. \n\nI hope you don't think I'm neglecting myself on purpose. I really am trying my best to keep going. Is that the sort of thing you needed to know about my weight and eating? I hope I'm answering your questions properly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I always go back there to check, just to be safe, because it feels so real in the moment. But I know it's just my mind being a little overtired. Just... just every once in a while. Maybe a couple of times a week? And only when it's very late and I've been staring at the same setting for hours. \n\nIt's not like it's a constant distraction or anything that's keeping me from my work. It's embarrassing to admit, but sometimes I do notice people looking a bit lost when I'm talking. \n\nLike, if a customer asks a simple question—just something like when their ring repair will be ready—I'll start to answer, and then somehow I'm talking about the invoices... and then maybe how the garden soil needs turning, and then suddenly I'm mentioning the mortgage papers. And I can just see it in their faces, you know? They're completely losing me. \n\nIn my head, it feels like all those things are connected in the moment, but when I see their expressions, I realize I must sound a bit scattered. I always try to pull it back and apologize, of course. I just want to be helpful to them. It's just stress making my thoughts jump around a little bit, right? I really hope I'm not making a terrible impression on people.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble expressing your thoughts in a coherent and logical manner, even when you're not feeling stressed or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, it's mostly just when things are busy, I think. I mean, I really try to be organized. But I guess... well, like I was saying before, that's kind of the main reason I decided to make an appointment. \n\nEven when I'm just sitting at the counter and it's quiet, a customer might ask something so simple, like... like when their ring repair will be ready. And I start to tell them Tuesday, but then I'm suddenly talking about the invoices on my desk, and then somehow I'm mentioning that the garden soil needs turning before it freezes, and... and then I'm talking about the mortgage papers. \n\nI don't mean to do it, I promise. I just see their faces kind of go blank, and I realize I've completely lost them. It's so embarrassing. I always apologize and try to get back on track. I just want to give them good customer service, you know? It's probably just normal aging, right? Or just having a lot on my plate? I really hope it doesn't make me sound foolish.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're having trouble sticking to the point in your conversations most of the time, or is it still just occasional?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, I know I just gave you that exact example about the ring repairs and the garden soil... I'm so sorry, I must sound like a broken record! I hope I'm not frustrating you by repeating myself. \n\nTo answer your question, um... I'd like to think it's still just occasional. I really do try my hardest to keep my conversations on track, I promise. It's definitely not every single time I speak to someone. \n\nBut... well, it happens enough that it's really the whole reason I came in to see you today. Just those moments where a customer asks a simple question, and my answer just spirals from the shop invoices all the way to the mortgage papers, and I can physically see them losing me. I hate making people feel confused when I'm just trying to be helpful. I really do try my best to be clear... it's just a little occasional, harmless rambling, right? I'm sure plenty of people do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are racing or difficult to control when you're having these conversations that go off on a detour?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... racing? I wouldn't say racing. That sounds a bit... well, a bit extreme, doesn't it? I really don't want you to think I'm completely out of control. \n\nIt's just... oh dear, I know I'm bringing up the exact same example again. I am so sorry, you must be getting so tired of hearing about the shop! But it really is the whole reason I came in to see you. When they ask that simple question about the ring repair, the thoughts about the invoices and the garden soil and the mortgage... they don't feel fast or scary in my head. They just sort of tumble out because they all feel perfectly connected to me in that second. \n\nIt's only when I see them getting lost that I realize I didn't quite control how it came out, and it got all jumbled. I really am trying my best to keep things simple for them, I promise. Does that make sense? I hope I'm answering your question the right way...\n\nDoctor: Do you think the way your thoughts seem to jump from one topic to another is related to any changes in your mood or emotional state?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... my mood? I... well, I suppose everyone gets a little down sometimes, right? I really hope you don't think I'm just sitting around feeling sorry for myself. I try so hard to keep a positive attitude, especially for the customers. \n\nBut... well, if I'm being completely honest—and I really want to give you the right answers—I have been feeling a bit... low. It's not a big deal, really, I'm sure it's just a phase. But most mornings lately, I wake up with this... this flat, heavy sadness. It's almost like all the color has just drained out of the house before I even leave for the shop. \n\nI try to shake it off, I promise I do. I don't want to be a burden or bring anyone else down. Do you think... do you think feeling a little blue like that could be making me jumble my words up? I just want to make sure I'm giving you what you need to figure this out. I'm doing okay, though, really.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been waking up with that flat, heavy sadness in the mornings?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, when you ask how long... I really don't want it to sound like I've just been wallowing, you know? I do try my absolute hardest to push through it every single day, I promise. \n\nBut, um... if I'm being completely honest with you... I suppose it's been with me for about a year and four months now? Give or take a little. It started back during this really long stretch when the pressure from the shop's mortgage just started feeling... well, a bit impossible to keep up with. \n\nI feel terrible admitting it's been going on for that long without me just fixing it myself. I really should have snapped out of it by now, shouldn't I? I hope you don't think I'm just weak or not trying hard enough to be happy. I really am trying my best to handle it all... is it okay that it's been that long? I hope I'm not a hopeless case or anything.\n\nDoctor: Does the flat, heavy sadness you wake up with in the mornings tend to stay with you throughout the day, or does it lift at some point?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I really try not to let it ruin the whole day. I wouldn't want you to think I just drag my feet around the shop from morning till night, feeling miserable. I put on a smile for the customers, I really do. \n\nBut... well, since you're asking, and I want to be a good patient and give you the right answer... that flat, heavy sadness I mentioned? The one where it feels like the color is just drained out of everything? It... it doesn't really go away. I mean, I push through it! I keep working and I do my job. But underneath, it just sort of sits there with me all day long. \n\nI'm so sorry, I know that sounds awful, and I really should be able to cheer myself up by lunchtime, right? I hope it doesn't make me sound terribly gloomy or ungrateful for the things I do have. I just... I can't seem to shake it off, no matter how hard I try. Does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining it alright for you.\n\nDoctor: Does your sadness ever improve at any point during the day?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, like I was saying just a moment ago... I really do try to put on a brave face, you know? I don't want to be a bother to anyone, especially the customers at the jewelry shop. \n\nBut... no, it doesn't really improve. I wake up most mornings with that flat, heavy sadness... the one where it feels like all the color has just drained right out of the house before I even leave for work. And it just sort of... stays. I hope you don't think I'm just repeating myself to be difficult, I just... I want to make sure I'm answering your question the right way. I really do try my best to be cheerful, though. Is it okay that I feel that way?\n\nDoctor: Does there ever come a point in the day where you feel like the sadness lifts or becomes less heavy, even if it's just for a little while?\n\nPatient: Oh, I'm so sorry, Doctor. I must be doing a terrible job of explaining things if you're having to ask me again. I feel awful making you repeat yourself. \n\nI really wish I could give you a happier answer, I truly do. I'd love to tell you that it lifts after a nice cup of tea or when I start organizing the displays. But... well, it's just like I was trying to say before. Most mornings I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness... it's like the color has just drained out of the house before I even get to the shop. And I'm afraid it just stays that heavy, even though I try my absolute hardest to look on the bright side for the rest of the afternoon. \n\nI'm so sorry to be such a broken record about it. Am I making sense now? I really just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to enjoy activities you used to like despite feeling sad?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I really try to keep busy, I promise I do. I don't want to sound like I'm just sitting around the shop doing nothing all day. I'm usually very dedicated to my work. \n\nBut... well, since you're asking about things I used to enjoy... I feel terrible admitting this, but... I used to just love hunting for old, vintage clasps, you know? And I used to spend hours planning these little jewelry-making classes for the community. It was always so much fun. But lately... oh, I hope this doesn't sound too lazy of me... lately, I just find myself mostly staring at the trays of beads and stones. It just feels like none of it is really worth starting right now. \n\nI'm so sorry, I know I should be more enthusiastic about my trade. I really hope you don't think poorly of me for letting things slide a bit. Does that answer your question okay? I'm really trying to give you exactly what you need to know.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear, I'm so sorry. I must have mumbled or not been clear enough just a moment ago. I really don't mean to make you ask the same thing twice, Doctor. \n\nBut, um, yes, like I was trying to say... I used to get so much joy out of hunting for old clasps and planning those little jewelry classes. I really prided myself on it. But lately... well, I mostly just find myself staring at the trays of beads. It just feels like none of it is really worth starting right now. \n\nI feel so guilty even saying it out loud. I know I should be more grateful for my work. Am I giving you the right kind of information? I really just want to make sure I'm helping you figure this all out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... weight? Well, I don't want you to think I'm not taking care of myself. I really do try to keep up with things, I promise. I know how important it is to eat right. \n\nBut... I suppose, if I'm being completely honest... my appetite has been a little bit off. For a few months now, really. I feel awful admitting it, because I know I should be cooking proper meals, but... some days I'll get to the evening and realize I've only had a cup of coffee and maybe a few crackers. It's not that I'm trying to lose weight or anything silly like that! It's just... lately, the thought of preparing food and eating it... it just seems like another huge task that I don't have the energy for. \n\nI hope you don't think I'm neglecting myself on purpose. I really am trying my best to keep going. Is that the sort of thing you needed to know about my weight and eating? I hope I'm answering your questions properly.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of at least two weeks where you felt depressed or lost interest in activities nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... two weeks?
